</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>A female <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> may have <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS as the result of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> or she may have the disorder as the result of a pathogenic variant inherited from her mother or, possibly, her father. (About 85%-95% of females with <i>PDHA1</i>-related primary PDCD have the disorder as the result of a <i>de novo</i> pathogenic variant.)</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Detailed evaluation of the parents and review of the extended family history may help to distinguish probands with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> from those with an inherited pathogenic variant. Molecular genetic testing of the mother (and possibly the father either concurrently or subsequently if the mother's testing is unrevealing) can help to determine if the pathogenic variant was inherited.</div></li></ul><p>
<b>Sibs of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a></b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The risk to sibs of a male <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> of inheriting an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> depends on the genetic status of the mother: if the mother of the proband has a pathogenic variant, the chance of transmitting it in each pregnancy is 50%.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The risk to sibs of a female <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> of inheriting a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> depends on the genetic status of the mother and the father: if the mother of the proband has an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS-related pathogenic variant, the chance of transmitting it in each pregnancy is 50%; if the father of the proband has a pathogenic variant, he will transmit it to all his daughters and none of his sons.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The risk that a sib who inherits an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> will have manifestations of the disorder cannot be fully predicted. The risk of manifestations is influenced by the sex of the sib and &#x02013; in the case of <i>PDHA1</i>-related primary PDCD &#x02013; the sex of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>, and, to an extent, the type of pathogenic variant segregating in the family (i.e., an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/insertion/">insertion</a>/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion/">deletion</a> pathogenic variant or a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a> pathogenic variant).</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Males who inherit an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> will be affected; females who inherit a pathogenic variant will be heterozygotes and their clinical manifestations may range from asymptomatic to as severely affected as <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/hemizygous/">hemizygous</a> males.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>As a result of the difference in <i>PDHA1</i> pathogenic variants typically identified in male and female probands, it is difficult to fully predict what clinical manifestations may occur in a <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/heterozygous/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">heterozygous</a> or <a href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/hemizygous/" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">hemizygous</a> sib who is a different sex from the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>. Theoretically, the female sibs of a male proband with a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/missense/">missense</a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> may be asymptomatic or have milder manifestations than the proband, whereas an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/insertion/">insertion</a>/<a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/deletion/">deletion</a> pathogenic variant in a female proband may not be compatible with life in a hemizygous male fetus. Conclusive data regarding these possibilities are not available, as there are few known families in which both male and female relatives are known to have a <i>PDHA1</i> pathogenic variant (with the exception of male probands born to asymptomatic heterozygous mothers).</div></li></ul></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> represents a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/simplex/">simplex</a> case and the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> cannot be detected in the leukocyte DNA of the mother (or, if the proband is female and the pathogenic variant cannot be detected in the leukocyte DNA of the mother or the father), the risk to sibs is presumed to be low but greater than that of the general population because of the possibility of parental <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gonadal-mosaicism/">gonadal mosaicism</a>.</div></li></ul><p>
<b>Offspring of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a></b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Males with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS have not been known to reproduce.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Each child of a female with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS has a 50% chance of inheriting the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>.</div></li></ul><p><b>Other family members.</b> The risk to other family members depends on the status of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>'s parents: if a parent has an <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/x-linked/">X-linked</a> LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>, the parent's family members may be at risk.</p></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Autosomal_Dominant_Inherit"><h3>Autosomal Dominant Inheritance &#x02013; Risk to Family Members</h3><p>
<b>Parents of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a></b>
</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>All probands reported to date with <i>DNM1L</i>-, <i>OPA1</i>-, <i>SLC25A4</i>-, or <i>SSBP1</i>-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> LSS whose parents have undergone <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/molecular-genetic-testing/">molecular genetic testing</a> have the disorder as the result of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>Molecular genetic testing is recommended for the parents of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> to evaluate their genetic status and inform <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> assessment.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> identified in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> is not identified in either parent and parental identity testing has confirmed biological maternity and paternity, the following possibilities should be considered:</div><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> has a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>The <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> inherited a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> from a parent with gonadal (or somatic and gonadal) <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/mosaicism/">mosaicism</a>. Note: Testing of parental leukocyte DNA may not detect all instances of <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/somatic-mosaicism/">somatic mosaicism</a> and will not detect a pathogenic variant that is present in the germ (gonadal) cells only.</div></li></ul></li></ul><p><b>Sibs of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>.</b> The risk to the sibs of the proband depends on the genetic status of the proband's parents:</p><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If a parent of the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> has the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>, the risk to the sibs of inheriting the variant is 50%.</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>If the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a> found in the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a> cannot be detected in the leukocyte DNA of either parent, the <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/recurrence-risk/">recurrence risk</a> to sibs is estimated to be 1% because of the possibility of parental <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gonadal-mosaicism/">gonadal mosaicism</a> [<a class="bk_pop" href="#leigh-nucl-ov.REF.zemet.2022.811">Zemet et al 2022</a>].</div></li></ul><p><b>Offspring of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/proband/">proband</a>.</b> Individuals with <i>DNM1L</i>-, <i>OPA1</i>-, <i>SLC25A4</i>-, or <i>SSBP1</i>-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> LSS have not been known to reproduce.</p><p><b>Other family members.</b> Given that all probands with <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/autosomal-dominant/">autosomal dominant</a> LSS reported to date have the disorder as a result of a <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/de-novo/"><i>de novo</i></a> <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>, the risk to other family members is presumed to be low.</p></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Related_Genetic_Counseling"><h3>Related Genetic Counseling Issues</h3><p><b>DNA banking.</b> Because it is likely that testing methodology and our understanding of genes, pathogenic mechanisms, and diseases will improve in the future, consideration should be given to banking DNA from probands in whom a molecular diagnosis has not been confirmed (i.e., the causative pathogenic mechanism is unknown). For more information, see <a class="bk_pop" href="#leigh-nucl-ov.REF.huang.2022.389">Huang et al [2022]</a>.</p></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Prenatal_Testing_and_Preim"><h3>Prenatal Testing and Preimplantation Genetic Testing</h3><p>Once the LSS-related <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/pathogenic-variant/">pathogenic variant</a>(s) have been identified in an affected family member, prenatal and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a> are possible.</p><p>Differences in perspective may exist among medical professionals and within families regarding the use of prenatal and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/preimplantation-genetic-testing/">preimplantation genetic testing</a>. While most health care professionals would consider use of prenatal and preimplantation genetic testing to be a personal decision, discussion of these issues may be helpful.</p></div></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Resources"><h2 id="_leigh-nucl-ov_Resources_">Resources</h2><p>
<i>The following disease-specific and/or umbrella support organizations and/or registries are listed for the benefit of individuals with this disorder and their families. Registry</a></div></li></ul></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Chapter_Notes"><h2 id="_leigh-nucl-ov_Chapter_Notes_">Chapter Notes</h2><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Author_Notes"><h3>Author Notes</h3><p><b>Prof Rahman, Prof Thorburn, and Dr Ball</b> are actively involved in clinical research regarding individuals with nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded Leigh syndrome spectrum (LSS). They would be happy to communicate with persons who have any questions regarding diagnosis of nuclear gene-encoded LSS or other considerations.</p><p>
<a href="https://iris.ucl.ac.uk/iris/browse/profile?upi=SRAHM75" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Professor Rahman's web page</a>
</p><p>
<a href="https://www.mcri.edu.au/users/professor-david-thorburn" ref="pagearea=body&amp;targetsite=external&amp;targetcat=link&amp;targettype=uri">Professor Thorburn's web page</a>
</p><p>Professor Rahman's research interests include identification of novel nuclear genes causing mitochondrial disease using a combination of approaches including homozygosity mapping and <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/exome/">exome</a> and genome <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/next-generation-sequencing/">next-generation sequencing</a>. Her group has identified a number of nuclear genes causing childhood-onset mitochondrial disorders, including genes involved in mitochondrial DNA maintenance and expression, complex I and complex IV function, and biosynthesis of coenzyme Q<sub>10</sub>. Other research interests aim to identify biomarkers and novel therapies for childhood mitochondrial disorders.</p><p>Professor Thorburn's research focuses on improving diagnosis, prevention, and treatment of mitochondrial energy generation disorders. This has included translating knowledge of mitochondrial DNA genetics into reproductive options for families, defining diagnostic criteria and epidemiology, and discovery of new "disease" genes through <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/genomic/">genomic</a>, multi-omic, and targeted functional testing. His group also uses pluripotent and other cellular models to understand pathogenic mechanisms and trial new treatment approaches.</p></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Acknowledgments"><h3>Acknowledgments</h3><p>The authors would like to acknowledge the support of clinicians, researchers, and patient advocacy groups for nuclear <a class="def" href="/sites/books/n/gene/glossary/def-item/gene/">gene</a>-encoded LSS. They would also like to acknowledge all the patients and families who have generously given their time and shared their experiences to advance the understanding of nuclear gene-encoded LSS.</p></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Revision_History"><h3>Revision History</h3><ul><li class="half_rhythm"><div>1 May 2025 (bp) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>16 July 2020 (bp) Comprehensive update posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>1 October 2015 (me) Review posted live</div></li><li class="half_rhythm"><div>17 February 2015 (sr) Original submission</div></li></ul></div></div><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.References"><h2 id="_leigh-nucl-ov_References_">References</h2><div id="leigh-nucl-ov.Literature_Cited"><h3>Literature Cited</h3><ul class="simple-list"><li class="half_rhythm"><div class="bk_ref" id="leigh-nucl-ov.REF.abrams.2015.926">Abrams

        $('.skiplink').each(function(i, item){
            var href = $($(item).attr('href'));
            href.attr('tabindex', '-1').addClass('skiptarget'); // ensure the target can receive focus

                event.preventDefault();
                $.scrollTo(href, 0, {
                    onAfter: function(){
                        href.focus();
                    }
                });
            });
        });
